Experiments show that SemiCodec outperforms fully discrete codecs in reconstruction quality and intelligibility while remaining competitive with continuous representations in acoustic fidelity and speaker preservation. Audio samples are available at https://pennyw1-se.github.io/SemiCodec-Demo-Page/</p></details> | <details><summary>5 pag...</summary><p>5 pages, 2 figures, 3 tables. Submitted to ICASSP 2027</p></details> |
| **[PRISM: Precision Forcing for Generalist Vision-Language-Action Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14704v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Coupling a pretrained vision-language model (VLM) with a diffusion or flow-matching action expert has become the mainstream vision-language-action (VLA) paradigm for embodied policies, yet they remain unreliable once a task demands precise manipulation, e.g., submillimeter cable insertion. We attribute this weakness to a training objective that averages flow-matching errors across contexts and flow times, allocating little supervision to the few decisive moments determining task success. Along the generative path, inference integrates a locally supervised velocity field, so equally penalized errors at different flow times can affect the executed action unequally. Consequently, precision-task performance deteriorates as physical tolerance tightens by orders of magnitude. We call this supervision imbalance across contexts and flow times Flow-Time Starvation. The remedy is to jointly align supervision allocation and representation learning with context-dependent precision demand, enabling the policy to recognize when greater precision is needed. We instantiate this precision-forcing strategy as Precision-aware Reweighting and Internalization for Skilled Manipulation (PRISM), which scores precision demand offline, strengthens flow supervision where errors are consequential and action signals are visible, and internalizes that demand in the VLM condition representation. Simulation and real-robot experiments show that PRISM preserves or improves general-task performance while raising mean end-to-end success from 38.3% to 86.7% across drive connector insertion, bimanual connector mating, and screw tightening, substantially extending the precision boundary of existing embodied policies without new sensors or observation modalities.</p></details> |  |

We further propose Gait-VL, an attribute-centric vision-language alignment framework that recognizes gait attributes by matching attribute-specific multi-frame visual evidence with textual class prototypes. On the visual side, attribute-specific learnable queries aggregate RGB features across spatial locations and frames into per-attribute visual embeddings. On the language side, diverse descriptions expanded by a large language model are combined with context about relevant body regions to construct textual class prototypes. Beyond attribute recognition, we evaluate the transferability of the attribute-pretrained visual encoder by using it to replace generic upstream large vision models in identityoriented gait recognition pipelines; attribute pre-training uses no identity classification or metric-learning objectives. | **[DEFT: Disentanglement-Enhanced Fine-Tuning for EEG Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14497v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>EEG foundation models (EFMs) have improved EEG representation learning by transferring knowledge from large-scale multi-subject pretraining to downstream tasks. | **[HiPPT: Physiology-Grounded Wearable PPG Representation Learning via Multi-Scope Text Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14489v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reusable wearable PPG representations should capture local pulse morphology and broader physiological regularities. | **[Prior-Informed Generalizable Low-Rank Representation Learning via Regularized Adversarial Reweighting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14414v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern learning problems increasingly leverage data from heterogeneous sources to improve robustness and generalizability under distribution shift, often with high-dimensional parameters admitting low-rank structure. Existing group distributionally robust optimization (group-DRO) approaches optimize against adverse source mixtures, but extending this principle to low-rank matrix estimation with a broad class of losses leads to a challenging nonconvex-concave problem for which methods and theory remain limited. We propose Prior-Informed generalizable Low-rank representation Learning via Adversarial Reweighting (PILLAR), which smooths the group-DRO objective by regularizing adversarial source weights toward a user-specified reference distribution while learning a shared low-rank representation. We further show that PILLAR improves on vanilla group DRO when the latter's excess conservativeness dominates source heterogeneity and prior misspecification. | **[Where to Look Matters: Rethinking Sub-Volume Sampling in 3D Medical Self-supervised Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13915v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Self-supervised learning on 3D medical images commonly relies on random sub-volume sampling during pretraining. | **[EchoTab: Learning Measurement-Grounded Representations for Echocardiographic Diagnosis through Tabular Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13599v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diagnostic information in multi-view medical imaging studies, like echocardiography, is distributed across views, each capturing different aspects of cardiac structure and function. | **[RARA: Robust Policy Fine-Tuning through Retrieval-Anchored Representation Alignment for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13578v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Pre-trained robot policies, such as Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models, provide a strong foundation for generalist robotic manipulation, but adapting them to new tasks with only a few demonstrations remains challenging. | **[MOSAIC: Multi-Scale Object-Centric Slot Adaptation with Image-Guided Prototype Conditioning for Remote Sensing Image Super-Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13547v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Pretrained diffusion Transformers provide powerful generative priors for image restoration, yet adapting them to structure-sensitive tasks remains challenging. CVAF-Net processes raw PPG as a temporal view and a feature sequence map (FSM) as a structured prior-guided view, and fuses the two representations through cross-view attention. The model was independently evaluated using 5-, 15-, and 30-s segments from three datasets: simulated pulse waves (3323 subjects), vasoconstriction provocation (79 subjects), and resting/cycling activities (10 subjects), and was compared with multiple machine learning and deep learning benchmarks. CVAF-Net outperformed most benchmark methods and achieved performance comparable to a state-of-the-art Transformer-based model, with a mean absolute error (MAE) of 0.19 L/min (MAPE: 3.95%) on simulated data and high accuracy in real-world settings (minimum MAE: 1.20 L/min). Plausibility analysis showed physiologically consistent CO estimates, with expected correlations with age ($ρ= -0.274$), heart rate ($ρ= 0.894$), and systemic vascular resistance ($ρ= -0.740$). | **[ITO: Multi-View Alignment and Training-Time Fusion for Image-Text Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02767v5)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Image--text contrastive pretraining has become a dominant paradigm for visual representation learning, yet existing methods often yield representations that remain partially organized by modality rather than by semantics. Crucially, the fusion module is discarded at inference, preserving the efficiency of standard dual-encoder architectures while incurring training-time overhead only. ## Graph Transformers
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Why don't Graph Transformers attend to the same token?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19324v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In large language models (LLMs), attention concentrates heavily on the first token. This 'attention sink' is believed to protect deep models against over-mixing. Even though graph transformers face a related pressure, we find that a SoTA graph transformer, GRIT, rarely forms sinks on molecules, and that it sinks less when trained on larger graphs, the opposite of the trend in LLMs. In principle, a graph can prevent sinks: we prove and show empirically that its symmetry and locality limit the attention any node can receive. On molecules, however, with distinguishable atoms and global attention, neither limit prevents sinks; what stops them lies in the model. We find that the attention scoring function is the main cause. Replacing GRIT's gated logit with a dot product raises its sink rate from 0.09 to 0.68 on identical graphs. In Graph Transformers using dot-product attention, removing the sink gives about the same error as removing a random node, while GRIT's most-attended nodes matter to its predictions. Attention sinks are therefore a property of dot-product attention rather than of Transformers. In our experiments, they form when dot-product scoring has a distinguishable node to concentrate on.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the Geometric Distributional Deep Learning (GDDL) workshop at NeurIPS 2026. 8 pages, 1 figure. | **[A finite-element-inspired bipartite graph learned simulator for manufacturability assessment in large-deformation sheet forming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22845v3)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Explicit dynamic finite element (FE) simulations are widely used for large-deformation analysis but remain expensive for design-space exploration. Mesh nodes and elements are represented as distinct graph entities connected by directed node-element edges, enabling prediction of nodal displacement increments and elemental deformation on their native domains. A within-subjects study with nine professional designers indicates that grounding conversation in a relationally structured, designer-specific semantic context improves context retention, personalized intent interpretation, and conversational depth while reducing cognitive load. These findings suggest that structuring a designer's expressed concepts and relations as a dynamic knowledge graph can preserve relational context that fades across turns, pointing toward a graph-grounded approach to long-term context management in LLM-based interaction.</p></details> |  |

| **[Lossy compression of weighted graph adjacency matrices by transform coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14834v1)** | 2026-07-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In this paper, we propose a compression framework for weighted graphs in which the graph topology is transmitted losslessly and edge weights are compressed lossily. The line graph transform allows us to regard edge weights as a graph signal defined on the line graph. Instead of transmitting the edge-weight vector, we first transform it with a graph filter bank on the line graph. Then, quantization and entropy coding are performed on the transformed coefficients of the edge weight vector. In addition to the lossy compression method, we formalize edge smoothness on the line graph and show that it serves as a measure of the difficulty of compression. The proposed smoothness measure can be easily calculated without converting to a line graph. This provides insight into the expected compression performance of a given weighted graph. Experiments on synthetic and real-world data validate the effectiveness of the proposed method by comparing it with existing matrix preprocessing methods.</p></details> |  |

As such, GenGNN uplifts local message-passing to challenge prevailing assumptions that performant discrete graph generation requires global attention or higher-order representations. | **[One Task, Many Interfaces: Simple Cross-Interface Consistency Training Improves LLM Agent Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20576v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Post-training binds the competence of an LLM agent to the interface it was trained on. Scoring each sample independently means interface randomization only broadens this dependence from a single interface to the training interface distribution, so out-of-distribution perturbations still degrade the agent. We propose Cross-Interface Policy Regularization (CIPR), a self-supervised objective that replaces action names with uninformative identifiers and presents each training sample under two interfaces in which equivalent actions are identified by construction. We evaluate CIPR in both supervised and reinforcement-learning post-training, where it improves robustness. On agentic function calling, held-out interface perturbations reduce the accuracy of a post-trained agent by nearly 25%, and CIPR recovers 10.5% while leaving accuracy on the unchanged interface intact.</p></details> |  |

Specifically, ICW transforms the holistic judgment over all criteria into a series of pairwise importance comparisons, checks and revises inconsistent comparisons, and derives the weights from the principal eigenvector of the comparison matrix. We train a 9B-parameter model that writes better prompts than state-of-the-art frontier models (gpt-5.6-sol and claude-opus-5) and prompt optimizers (GEPA and TextGrad) on 2/3 benchmarks. Although the model is never explicitly trained to iterate on prompts, this capability emerges at a level that matches or beats frontier models. The learned prompting capability also generalizes, with the trained model improving task models 6x larger than the one it trained with, including ones from entirely different model families, and matching claude-opus-5 on a fourth benchmark held out from training.</p></details> | 25 pages, 6 figures |
| **[Automated Standardization of Legacy Biomedical Metadata Using an Ontology-Constrained LLM Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08552v4)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Descriptive scientific metadata in public repositories are often incomplete and inconsistent with community standards and ontologies, limiting data FAIRness. Large language models (LLMs) offer a promising approach to automatically standardizing such metadata when the LLM is provided with knowledge of the relevant standards in machine-actionable form (e.g., as metadata templates from systems such as the CEDAR Workbench). Entry of metadata standards through prompt engineering offers only fixed snapshots of the relevant standards, however, and it relies on an LLM's pretrained knowledge to interpret and satisfy constraints related to the standard. We evaluate whether giving an LLM access to metadata specifications and authoritative terminology at runtime improves automated metadata standardization. We present ARMS, a tool-augmented LLM agent that retrieves complete CEDAR metadata templates and dynamically queries authoritative biomedical terminology services at execution time. We compared ARMS with a prompt-based approach on 839 legacy metadata records from the Human BioMolecular Atlas Program (HuBMAP), using expert-standardized records as the reference standard. ARMS outperformed the prompt-based approach, increasing precision from 0.56 to 0.93 and recall from 0.51 to 0.85, with improvements across all field categories and assay types. The largest gains occurred for ontology-constrained fields, where precision increased from 0.36 to 0.92. Our findings show that access to appropriate metadata standards can substantially improve LLM-based standardization of legacy metadata. ARMS improves metadata standardization by giving LLMs runtime access to the authoritative resources that define valid metadata. Representation of metadata standards in machine-actionable form enhances the task of rectifying legacy metadata using LLM technology, especially when those standards can be queried dynamically.</p></details> |  |
| **[Anticipatory Evaluation of Language Models: Predicting LLM Benchmark Scores from Descriptions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20645v4)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern ML research increasingly faces an evaluation bottleneck: building benchmarks and running experiments is costly, slowing iteration. Experiments with frontier LLMs show that accurate regression remains challenging, while pairwise predictions can recover relative performance ordering under matched settings. | **[Can LLMs Access their World Knowledge for Event Prediction?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19021v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent work has questioned whether large language models (LLMs) can be useful for temporal event prediction. To test this, we introduce DescribeEvent, a benchmark of ~150K events across seven datasets and six domains with event-level descriptions. We find that capable LLMs substantially benefit from descriptions and symbolic labels, outperforming description-free variants and non-LLM baselines, while weaker models gain little. These results suggest that accessing LLM world knowledge for event prediction is an interface problem, and that event descriptions offer a practical way to elicit the knowledge LLMs already encode.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to Findings of EMNLP 2026</p></details> |
| **[PixelMoE: Scaling Pixel-Space Diffusion with LLM-Style Mixture-of-Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18857v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Pixel-space diffusion models provide an appealing alternative to latent-space diffusion models by directly modeling raw images without relying on a learned tokenizer. Building on this finding, we introduce PixelMoE, a pixel-space generative framework that sparsely scales model capacity with standard LLM-style MoE, without diffusion-specific routing mechanisms or auxiliary routing objectives. Specifically, PixelMoE combines standard Token-Choice (TC) routing with fine-grained routed experts for input-adaptive sparse computation, a shared expert for capturing common representations, and a bias-based load-balancing strategy. To complement sparse capacity scaling, PixelMoE further introduces Pixel-Space Hierarchical Flow Matching, which varies spatial resolution along the denoising trajectory from global structure formation to fine-grained detail refinement. At comparable active parameter counts, PixelMoE consistently outperforms its dense counterparts across model scales, demonstrating effective sparse capacity scaling in pixel space. On class-conditional ImageNet-1K, PixelMoE achieves FID scores of 1.47 at 256x256 and 1.53 at 512x512 without representation alignment or perceptual losses, comparing favorably with leading pixel-space and latent-space generative models. | **[Where Do LLM Ensemble Gains Come From? Under either non-diverse control, overall candidate gain (CIV) splits algebraically into an additional-candidate contrast (DSV) and a candidate-context contrast (CCV); the split, but not CIV, depends on the chosen control. On MultiHopRAG model diversity (k=2), the repetition gap Rep=-8.33 pp [-13.25, -3.75] quantifies the difference between gains measured against Duplicate and Single-slot. On a matched FRAMES stronger-panel extension (n=200, k=3, aggregator output limit 4096), paired Delta Rep=+9.67 pp [+6.00, +13.50]; within panels, Rep changes sign (original -6.33; stronger +3.33). All four judges clear the plan's raw-agreement criterion of .7; only Gemini reaches .7 on chance-corrected $κ$. Across the three primary events, machine reports reproduce 5.4-11.0% of same-day official figures. Across those same events, raw exact-number matching links 14.3-19.6% of official figures and 85.4-90.1% of machine figures to the social stream; manual audit confirms 45/50 declared absences, 4/25 official matches, and 24/25 machine matches semantically. | **[Energy-Aware Scheduling for Serverless LLM Serving on Shared GPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30391v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As LLM inference becomes a major cloud workload, its growing energy footprint makes cluster-wide energy optimization increasingly important. As a result, minimizing energy requires coordinated scheduling across request placement, runtime resource adaptation, and workload consolidation. We present Festina, a profiling-guided, power-aware control plane to minimize cluster-wide energy for serverless LLM serving. Unlike common global-local schedulers that focus on throughput or tail latency, Festina makes energy-first decisions by jointly coordinating request placement, SM partitioning, and GPU operating points under TTFT/TBT SLOs. In our system, a lightweight global scheduler performs fast, SLO-safe, energy-aware placement using constant-time lookups from offline profiles and GPU state summaries. On each GPU, a phase-aware local scheduler continuously adapts task batching and compute resources to minimize power consumption. Festina further performs energy-aware workload consolidation to reduce GPUs' static power consumption via SLO-aware migration. Comparison with four SOTA LLM serving systems and one DVFS-augmented system demonstrates that Festina reduces energy consumption by up to 56% while maintaining parity in SLO attainment (within a 2% margin)</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages body and 4 pages appendix, 19 pages total. Accepted to the 2026 ACM SIGOPS Annual Technical Conference (SIGOPS ATC 2026)</p></details> |
| **[FinTradeBench: A Financial Reasoning Benchmark for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.19225v6)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Financial analysis requires reasoning over heterogeneous signals, including company fundamentals from regulatory filings and trading signals derived from market prices. However, existing financial benchmarks focus almost exclusively on regulatory filings, neglecting market dynamics and cross-signal interactions. To holistically evaluate financial reasoning, FinTradeBench includes questions in three reasoning categories: fundamentals-focused, trading-signal-focused, and hybrid questions that require cross-signal reasoning. Our benchmark starts with 150 expert-authored seed questions, which are then scaled to 1400. To ensure reliable evaluation, we create a Core400 subset in which every item is expert-reviewed. In zero-shot (No-RAG) response generation, we observe that all models perform substantially worse on trading-focused questions compared to fundamental-focused questions. To identify failure causes, we evaluate LLMs under controlled settings including retrieval-based baselines, tool-assisted computation, oracle conditions, and time-series-specialized pipelines. LLMs' capabilities to do computation and evidence selection contribute most to reasoning failures. Additionally, retrieval significantly improves trading-signal reasoning but does not improve fundamental reasoning, where identifying relevant signals remains the dominant failure mode. Finally, rather than relying on a small, fine-tuned model for time-series reasoning, it is better to summarize the time-series data and leverage LLMs' reasoning capabilities.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages main text, 36 pages total (including references and appendix). 9 figures, 31 tables. Preprint under review. Code and data will be made available upon publication</p></details> |
| **[LLM-as-Jev: LLMs Are Already Jev-Style Decision Models -- When and How to Fine-Tune Them](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02076v3)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Jev-style decision models have gained surging traction by making decision-making dramatically faster and more efficient, returning categorical probability distributions over predefined options without generating free-form text. We present LLM-as-Jev, an architecture-preserving framework offering both a training-free inference recipe and a fine-tuning objective with KL-divergence anchors. Across Qwen3.5-4B and Qwen3-0.6B, we find that modern LLMs are inherently effective decision models: off the shelf, frozen models match or exceed existing training-free readouts on the same 4B backbone, scale to arbitrary option counts, and natively handle multimodal decisions. They also seamlessly inherit the backbone's thinking mode: while Jev was introduced as a System 1 model, "Thinking Jev" leverages budgeted test-time reasoning to boost accuracy on challenging tasks without training. Fine-tuning provides targeted gains for smaller models and complex routing, while our KL anchors prevent behavioral degradation in general text generation.</p></details> |  |
| **[OracleProto: A Reproducible Framework for Benchmarking LLM Forecasting via Knowledge Cutoffs and Temporal Masking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.03762v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Forecasting is a composite capability encompassing information gathering, evidence integration, situational assessment, and decision-making. Through model knowledge cutoff constraints and temporal masking, OracleProto constructs a temporal sandbox for real-world web retrieval, audits information boundaries through leakage detection, and reconstructs resolved events as forecasting instances. Discrete answer normalization and hierarchical scoring enable unified evaluation. We evaluate 27 model-configuration pairs on 300 FutureX-Past-derived questions over 24,300 prediction trials, assessing forecasting quality, sampling stability, and cost efficiency under controlled information boundaries. An audit finds only 1.37% residual leakage in retrieval results after temporal masking and leakage filtering. OracleProto retains resolved forecasting tasks as reusable evaluation resources under explicit temporal boundaries, supporting continual accumulation of forecasting datasets and systematic evaluation of forecasting capabilities. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/OracleProto-2E3A/.</p></details> |  |
| **[What Users See Is Not What Models Read: Split-View PDFs in Document-to-LLM Supply Chains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15020v3)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Document-to-LLM applications typically read uploaded PDFs by first translating them into text through an extraction layer whose output is not ordinarily exposed to users. ## graph neural network
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Revisiting Simple Baselines for Relational Deep Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20534v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Relational deep learning has emerged as a powerful paradigm for learning end-to-end predictive models over relational databases by representing them as temporal heterogeneous graphs. Recent advances based on graph neural networks and Transformers have achieved strong performance across diverse domains. However, the effectiveness of simpler relational baselines remains underexplored, making it difficult to assess how much architectural complexity is needed to achieve strong predictive performance. Motivated by the observation that predictive signals arise from target attributes, relational attributes, and historical labels, we revisit how effectively these signals can be exploited using simple propagation methods. We adapt fixed low-pass feature propagation to relational entity graphs and train an MLP for prediction. The resulting predictions are further refined using Correct-and-Smooth, a graph-based label propagation algorithm that incorporates historical labels over task-specific meta-graphs connecting target entities. Across classification and regression tasks on RelBench, an MLP trained on neighborhood-filtered features outperforms one using only target-entity features on most tasks and recovers a substantial portion of the performance of heterogeneous GNNs. Correct-and-Smooth provides further gains from historical labels, although applying it requires a separately constructed meta-graph because connections among target entities are not directly specified by the relational entity graph. Together, our studies revisit the effectiveness of established propagation methods as simple RDL baselines while identifying canonical meta-graph construction as a key challenge for effective label propagation.</p></details> |  |
| **[MAEM: Mitigating Recency Bias via a Replay-Free, Capacity-Controlled Dynamic Mixture of Experts for Class-Incremental Graph Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20238v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) excel on graph-structured data but suffer catastrophic forgetting when classes arrive sequentially. In class-incremental graph learning, predictions are often dominated by recently learned classes, causing severe recency bias. Dynamic expansion can isolate knowledge across sessions, but many methods assume task-incremental settings and require task identities at test time to select branches or heads---information unavailable in class-incremental learning. Learnable routers offer an alternative, yet continual fine-tuning the router on new data can bias routing toward recent components. We propose MAEM, a replay-free, capacity-controlled dynamic mixture-of-experts framework for class-incremental graph learning. MAEM builds representations with Simplified Graph Convolution (SGC) and trains a dedicated expert for each new session, then freezes it to protect prior knowledge. For routing without task IDs or a shared trainable router, each expert uses a masked autoencoder (MAE); test nodes are assigned to the expert with the lowest reconstruction error, reducing routing-level recency bias. When the expert pool reaches a fixed capacity, MAEM merges the least-used experts via Git Re-Basin alignment and further distills them into a fused expert using topology-aware pseudo data synthesized from MAE distributions (DeepInversion-style), without storing or revisiting historical graph data. On four class-incremental graph benchmarks, MAEM mitigates recency bias, outperforms strong continual graph learning baselines, and keeps model capacity bounded under strict replay-free learning.</p></details> |  |
| **[Design Space Exploration of Reinforcement Learning Configurations for Post-Legalization Placement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20224v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) and Reinforcement Learning (RL) have recently been explored for VLSI placement optimization, but their effectiveness for post-legalization placement refinement remains unclear. This paper presents a systematic design space exploration of a GNN-Proximal Policy Optimization (GNN-PPO) framework across three ISPD2015 benchmarks. We evaluate 18 configurations covering reward functions, GNN architectures, action spaces, candidate-set sizes, and training strategies. We analyse the results we obtain to identify pitfalls and to assess the prospects and opportunities of the RL methods for post-legalization placement. On top of that we propose a design classification approach to guide the design decisions of RL models.</p></details> |  |
| **[HGRP: Heterogeneous Graph Neural Network for Routability Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20209v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Routability prediction enables rapid evaluation of placement quality without routing, providing effective feedback for routability-driven placement optimization. Recent machine learning methods have achieved efficient routability prediction through data-driven approach. However, their prediction performance highly depends on the capability of input representations to capture essential circuit information. To address the limitations of existing circuit representations in simultaneously preserving logical topology, physical layout and routing resource information, this paper proposes HGRP, a heterogeneous graph neural network for routability prediction. HGRP models the cells, nets, and routing grids as distinct types of nodes and explicitly represents topological connectivity, spatial mapping, and grid adjacency, thereby constructing a heterogeneous graph representation that preserves multi-level circuit semantics. Based on the representation, a heterogeneous message passing mechanism is developed to enable information interaction and feature fusion among distinct types of nodes, effectively learning the complex correlations between circuit structures and routing behaviors. Experimental results on the CircuitNet dataset show that HGRP improves F1-score by 18.69\% relative improvements compared with the state-of-the-art in the congestion prediction task. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/ShenShan123/HGPR.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by ASP-DAC2027. Camera-Ready version</p></details> |
| **[Causally-Constrained Probabilistic Forecasting for Time-Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17998v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Anomaly detection in multivariate time series is a central challenge in industrial monitoring, as failures frequently arise from complex temporal dynamics and cross-sensor interactions. While recent deep learning models, including graph neural networks and Transformers, have demonstrated strong empirical performance, most approaches remain primarily correlational and offer limited support for causal interpretation and root-cause localization. This study introduces a causally-constrained probabilistic forecasting framework which is a Causally Guided Transformer (CGT) model for multivariate time-series anomaly detection, integrating an explicit time-lagged causal graph prior with deep sequence modeling. For each target variable, a dedicated forecasting block employs a hard parent mask derived from causal discovery to restrict the main prediction pathway to graph-supported causes, while a latent Gaussian head captures predictive uncertainty. To leverage residual correlational information without compromising the causal representation, a shadow auxiliary path with stop-gradient isolation and a safety-gated blending mechanism is incorporated to suppress non-causal contributions when reliability is low. Anomalies are identified using negative log-likelihood scores with adaptive streaming thresholding, and root-cause variables are determined through per-dimension probabilistic attribution and counterfactual clamping. Experiments on the ASD and SMD benchmarks indicate that the proposed method achieves state-of-the-art detection performance, with F1-scores of 96.19% on ASD and 95.32% on SMD, and enhances variable-level attribution quality. These findings suggest that causal structural priors can improve both robustness and interpretability in detecting deep anomalies in multivariate sensor systems.</p></details> | <details><summary>This ...</summary><p>This work is currently under review for possible publication in the IEEE Access journal. All intellectual property rights are retained by IEEE</p></details> |
| **[Njord: A Probabilistic Graph Neural Network for Ensemble Ocean Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15470v3)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Ocean dynamics are inherently chaotic, yet existing machine learning ocean models produce only deterministic forecasts. We introduce Njord, a probabilistic data-driven model for ocean forecasting, applicable to both global and regional domains. Njord combines a deep latent variable framework with a graph neural network architecture, enabling sampling each forecast step in a single forward pass. We apply Njord globally at 0.25° resolution and regionally to the Baltic Sea at 2 km resolution. To scale to these large ocean grids we introduce K-means cluster meshes that adapt to irregular sea surface geometry. Experiments demonstrate strong performance on both domains compared to deterministic machine learning baselines, while also providing uncertainty estimates from the sampled ensemble forecasts. On the global OceanBench benchmark, Njord achieves the lowest errors on average across upper-ocean variables when evaluated against real-world observations, with the largest improvements in surface temperature prediction.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026. Code is available at https://github.com/deinal/njord</p></details> |
| **[Privacy-Preserving GNN Inference on Financial Transaction Graphs via Homomorphic Encryption](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18054v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Anti-money laundering (AML) in financial networks requires modeling transaction patterns that span multiple institutions. In practice, however, banks cannot share account and transaction features due to strict privacy regulations. We propose a privacy-preserving graph neural network (GNN) inference framework tailored to this multi-bank setting. A central server holds the graph connectivity and pretrained model weights in plaintext, while each bank keeps its transaction features encrypted. Inference is then carried out entirely on encrypted features under the CKKS homomorphic encryption scheme, so that no plaintext transaction features or predictions are exposed to the server. The key to making this practical is an edge-centric GNN architecture, wherein message passing is expressed through operations on node--edge incidence matrices. This formulation not only preserves the edge-attributed multigraph structure of transaction networks but also reduces every aggregation step to plaintext--ciphertext matrix products. Since CKKS can evaluate only polynomial circuits, we also approximate each nonlinearity with a composite polynomial of the sign function, which we calibrate per activation site. On the IBM AML benchmark, our model achieves performance competitive with plaintext baselines, and with a consumer-grade GPU, classifies each queried transaction end-to-end in as little as 5.31 seconds, while per-transaction latency can be amortized to 1.65 seconds when optimized for throughput.</p></details> |  |
| **[DeTox-Fed: Detecting Toxic Conversations in the Fediverse with Federated Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21054v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rise of decentralized social networks (DSNs), and in particular the rapid uptake of the Fediverse (e.g., Pleroma, Mastodon, Lemmygrad), introduces new challenges in content moderation. Independent instances host their own data, follow different moderation policies, and often observe only partial views of conversations. We present DeTox-Fed, a federated graph-learning framework for detecting toxic conversations in DSNs without requiring instances to share raw conversations or moderation labels. Each instance constructs a local conversation graph, where nodes represent conversation trees and edges capture shared user participation across conversations. A Graph Neural Network is then trained in a federated learning setup, allowing instances to collaboratively learn a toxicity classifier while preserving data locality. Unlike text-only moderation approaches, DeTox-Fed combines conversational structure, user-interaction patterns, conversation-level statistics, and aggregate sentiment signals. We evaluate the framework on a large Pleroma conversation dataset and a second one from Mastodon, showing that it achieves stable toxic conversation detection under limited local labels, partial client participation, and varying moderation thresholds. Our results indicate that federated graph-based moderation is a promising direction for semi-automated moderation in decentralized social networks.</p></details> | <details><summary>This ...</summary><p>This revised version substantially extends the experimental evaluation by incorporating a Mastodon dataset, additional LLM models (both lightweight and top-tier), and including GraphNLI as an experimental baseline</p></details> |
| **[Gauge-Invariant, Parameter-Insensitive Regularization for Potential Recovery from Flow on Directed Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13609v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recovering a latent potential from observed flow on a directed graph (a discrete Poisson problem with Dirichlet boundaries) is ill-posed, and the standard fix backfires: ridge regularization shrinks toward a gauge-meaningless origin, collapsing and reversing the recovered ordering ($+0.81\to-0.42$ rank correlation against a planted ground truth). The gauge-invariant graph Dirichlet energy removes the hazard and delivers parameter-insensitivity: the estimate is stable across four orders of magnitude in $λ$, whereas ridge inverts the ordering for every $λ>0$. We prove the reduced solve is SPD and preserves dynamic range exactly where ridge collapses it, and localize absorbing boundaries from flow alone via a Poisson residual. The $H^1$ seminorm is classical; what is new is the gauge diagnosis, the parameter-insensitivity it buys, and an ablation showing the result is robust to the extraction method. On three public clickstream corpora the gauge-invariant estimate retains $28$--$41\%$ of the interior dynamic range while ridge collapses to as little as $0.2\%$. The same gauge invariance carries into graph neural networks -- neutralizing the constant mode per layer prevents the oversmoothing that collapses a deep directed GCN -- linking this classical inverse problem to a central question in graph learning.</p></details> | <details><summary>19 pa...</summary><p>19 pages, 6 figures, Proceedings of the Fifth Learning on Graphs Conference (LoG 2026), PMLR 338</p></details> |
| **[On the Impact of Graph Neural Networks in Recommender Systems: A Topological Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.07384v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In recommender systems, user-item interactions can be modeled as a bipartite graph, where user and item nodes are connected by undirected edges. This graph-based view has motivated the rapid adoption of graph neural networks (GNNs), which often outperform collaborative filtering (CF) methods such as latent factor models, deep neural networks, and generative strategies. Yet, despite their empirical success, the reasons why GNNs offer systematic advantages over other CF approaches remain only partially understood. This monograph advances a topology-centered perspective on GNN-based recommendation. We argue that a comprehensive understanding of these models' performance should consider the structural properties of user-item graphs and their interaction with GNN architectural design. To support this view, we introduce a formal taxonomy that distills common modeling patterns across eleven representative GNN-based recommendation approaches and consolidates them into a unified conceptual pipeline. We further formalize thirteen classical and topological characteristics of recommendation datasets and reinterpret them through the lens of graph machine learning. Using these definitions, we analyze the considered GNN-based recommender architectures to assess how and to what extent they encode such properties. Building on this analysis, we derive an explanatory framework that links measurable dataset characteristics to model behavior and performance. Taken together, this monograph re-frames GNN-based recommendation through its topological underpinnings and outlines open theoretical, data-centric, and evaluation challenges for the next generation of topology-aware recommender systems.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted for publication in Foundations and Trends in Information Retrieval</p></details> |
| **[Blackknife: Query-Free Black-Box Rewiring Attacks on Heterogeneous Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.29240v3)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Heterogeneous graph neural networks (HGNNs) perform well on graphs with multiple node and relation types, but their robustness to attacks with restricted structural access remains insufficiently understood. We evaluate both forward prediction from marker deformation to contact localisation and inverse inference from contact configuration to marker deformation. The validated FEM achieves sub-0.5 mm error in more than 90% of validation cases, while the learned GNN surrogate reaches a mean absolute error of 0.32 mm with millisecond-scale inference, compared with minute-scale FEM simulation. | **[KARAMEL: Knowledge-Aware Ranking for Multimodal Entity Linking in the Arts via Heterogeneous Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16502v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Discovering and disambiguating entities from multimodal and heterogeneous data is a key challenge in discovery science, where relevant knowledge is often implicit and distributed across modalities. To benchmark multimodal discovery in this setting, we introduce WikiMuSA (Wikidata-based Multimodal Semantic data for Art), a new dataset that links artworks to Wikidata entities via images, textual descriptions, and structured contextual knowledge. | **[CryptoFlowBench: Benchmarking Multi-Hop Reasoning on Blockchains for Cryptocurrency Fund Tracing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16449v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Pseudonymous accounts and complex smart-contract ecosystems substantially hinder the tracing of illicit cryptocurrency fund flows. We therefore propose ChainSleuth, a graph-tool-augmented agent that iteratively reasons, invokes graph tools, and updates its tracing trajectory, enabling adaptive information acquisition and recovery from erroneous paths. ChainSleuth achieves notable improvements on multi-hop tasks, demonstrating the potential of agentic graph reasoning for cryptocurrency fund investigation.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 2 figures. Currently under review</p></details> |
| **[Imitation Learning of Avoidance Modes for Efficient Multimodal Model Predictive Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16326v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal model predictive control (MM-MPC) avoids local minima among moving obstacles by solving one optimal control problem (OCP) per combination of avoidance sides, so its computational cost grows exponentially with the number of decision-critical obstacles. We propose SideNet-MPC, which replaces this enumeration with a mode decision learned by imitating MM-MPC as an expert. A graph neural network is trained to predict the expert's minimum-cost mode, one avoidance side per obstacle. Its edges encode, for each obstacle pair, the gap predicted for the moment the vehicle first reaches either obstacle, so a neighbor that blocks a preferred passage can reverse the proposed side. The expert's selection rule compares its minimum-cost mode with the previously executed mode; SideNet-MPC keeps this rule and substitutes the network proposal for the minimum-cost mode. Hence at most two OCPs are solved per control period, and every applied input still comes from an OCP with control barrier function constraints rather than from the network. In $100$ seed-matched closed-loop Monte Carlo runs of mixed traffic, SideNet-MPC reaches $93\%$ of the expert's success rate and needs \SI{18.6}{ms} per control period on a single thread, 48\% less than the expert's \SI{35.5}{ms} on sixteen parallel threads. In a controlled gap sweep, its proposed side reverses in the same gap interval as the expert's, whereas proposers without pairwise edges, trained on the same data, never reverse and all deadlock at the blocked gates of a deterministic scene.</p></details> | <details><summary>This ...</summary><p>This work has been submitted to an Elsevier journal for possible publication</p></details> |

We then incorporate graph neural networks with graph convolution operations into the artificial neural network approximator of the DRL agent's action-value function. | **[Learning based Scalable Multi-Objective Scheduling of Workflows across UD-IaaS-FaaS Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15886v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Workflow scheduling in user device-centric edge-cloud computing has emerged as a challenging problem due to the need to jointly optimize execution performance, energy consumption, and monetary cost. The formulation models the workflow execution mechanism by incorporating cumulative capacity constraints for IaaS resources and elastic resource provisioning for FaaS. To overcome the intractability of MILP, we propose a learning-based framework that combines graph neural networks, surrogate performance modeling, and a multi-sample REINFORCE approach. The proposed approach is workflow-aware, leveraging task pairing to improve data locality and structured task placement decisions. Agent training is organized into three stages: small-scale learning of UD-IaaS trade-offs, intermediate locality aware co-location, and large-scale handling of parallelism and aggregation bottlenecks in workflows. The proposed framework is validated through real-world experiments using the Pegasus Montage workflow on a custom UD-IaaS-FaaS testbed comprising a user device, multiple IaaS nodes, and serverless FaaS instances. | **[A Dependency-Aware Multi-GPU Training Framework for Temporal Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15730v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Temporal graph neural networks (TGNNs) achieve superior performance in many dynamic graph learning tasks. These models use node state vectors (i.e., memories) to capture and record graph changes (i.e., events), and then exploit these memories to assist prediction. However, this memory-centric design makes multi-GPU training challenging because predictions for later events often depend on memory updates from earlier events. To maintain model quality, existing multi-GPU TGNN training schemes strictly preserve all event dependencies, which limits parallelism and causes expensive cross-GPU memory synchronization. In this work, we observe that many of these dependencies can be handled more flexibly without harming training quality. Specifically, (a) dependencies in TGNN training have high locality in both time and graph topology, (b) the short-range dependencies missed within a large batch can often be recovered cheaply, and (c) many memory updates are not immediately needed. Based on these observations, we propose DepBloom to accelerate multi-GPU TGNN training with three techniques: (1) temporally bounded spatial partitioning to reduce unnecessary cross-GPU synchronization and improve parallelism; (2) intra-batch memory refinement to recover missing short-range temporal dependencies inside enlarged batches; and (3) critical-path optimization to prioritize urgent memory updates while deferring non-critical work during multi-GPU pipeline execution. Experimental results across representative models and diverse datasets show that DepBloom achieves an average speedup of 2.84$\times$ and up to 5.16$\times$ over state-of-the-art methods, while maintaining comparable or better model accuracy.</p></details> |  |

This paper proposes a deep learning framework for the recovery of missing boundary information using an attention-guided graph neural network (GNN) surrogate. The results show that GNN-BEM provides a feasible computational framework for boundary information recovery and fast interior physical field reconstruction for partial differential equations under complex boundary conditions.</p></details> |  |
| **[ATLAS: A Foundation Neural Sampler for Amorphous Materials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19198v2)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Amorphous materials exhibit exceptional mechanical and functional properties, yet their rugged energy landscapes are notoriously difficult to sample. Here, we introduce ATLAS, an efficient sampler that learns a diffusion process to generate Boltzmann-distributed amorphous structures directly from a target energy function. Parameterized by an equivariant graph neural network, ATLAS generalizes across system size, temperature, and composition. By exploiting the time reversal of the diffusion process, it enables efficient estimation of thermodynamic quantities and steering toward target observables. In two-dimensional Kob-Andersen systems, ATLAS reproduces parallel tempering Markov chain Monte Carlo structural distributions, free energies and entropies, achieving below 0.2% free energy error in the low-temperature glass regime with over 500-fold fewer energy evaluations. In Cu-Zr and Cr-Co-Ni metallic glasses, ATLAS recovers experimentally observed short-range-order trends and steers structures toward prescribed order parameters and optimized bulk moduli. Moreover, composition-amortized pretraining outperforms composition-specific training from scratch, reduces inverse-design costs by several hundred-fold, and enables sampling with expensive universal machine learning interatomic potentials. Coupled to a large language model agent, ATLAS searches an eight-element space for high-entropy metallic glasses balancing stiffness and ductility, identifying a converged Pareto frontier within 480 oracle evaluations. This paper proposes Seq-BnB, a sequence-structured framework that learns branching and node selection from a shared global representation of the evolving BnB process. Seq-BnB serializes node creations, node updates, node-selection events, and branching events into typed tokens, producing a causal event sequence of the search trajectory. A self-attention encoder summarizes this global search history, a bipartite graph neural network encodes the current LP relaxation, and context-conditioned decision heads score branching variables and open nodes. Across diverse battery sizes (15-40 kWh) and customer demand distributions, GREAT outperforms a strong HGS-based EVRP heuristic in most tested settings, achieving energy reductions of up to 8.78% while reducing runtime by a factor of 60. To address these challenges, we propose DeKoR, an efficient framework for GNN and LLM knowledge fusion. To enable efficient knowledge transfer, DeKoR decomposes LLM knowledge into complementary local and global views and uses the LLM as an offline teacher to distill them into lightweight experts. However, this choice cannot be applied to a large class of scientific computing problems where data are represented on unstructured meshes, for example simulations on domains with curved boundaries. Such data typically require graph neural network architectures, which are more expensive to train and, in the form currently used, encode information differently from their CNN counterparts, thus limiting the portability of physics emulators between these data regimes. In this work we introduce Mesh-Native Convolution (MNC), a locality-aware tokenisation layer that encodes unstructured mesh-based data in a way that is analogous to CNNs. We prove that the resulting elastic feasible set is contractible, that the AC-OPF minimizers remain minimizers of the elastic problem above an explicit penalty threshold, and that projecting an approximate solution back onto the AC-OPF feasible set is well posed. We release all models, data, and code so that the community can build on a shared starting point for AC-OPF.</p></details> | 19 pages |
| **[Uncertainty Quantification Is Indispensable for Reliable Connectome-Based Graph Learning: A Narrative Review and Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08353v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While graph neural networks (GNNs) have shown substantial promise in connectome-based diagnostic classification, deterministic models inevitably suppress pipeline-induced noise and model ambiguities, yielding overconfident predictions. Although uncertainty quantification (UQ) is widely adopted in voxel-level segmentation, its role in connectomic graph learning remains largely unaddressed. This paper presents a comprehensive narrative review of UQ frameworks tailored to connectome graph learning alongside an empirical case study demonstrating the perils of uncalibrated predictions. We delineate sources of aleatoric and epistemic uncertainty across neuroimaging pipelines and review prominent UQ paradigms, from Bayesian approximations and ensemble methods to evidential learning and conformal prediction. In our case study, a temporal Graph Attention Network (GAT) trained on dynamic functional connectivity (dFC) matrices from the SUDMEX CONN dataset achieves 80.0% diagnostic accuracy (F1 = 0.794) for Cocaine Use Disorder. However, a post-hoc uncertainty audit via Monte Carlo dropout reveals severe overconfidence (ECE = 0.127), with misclassified subjects assigned prediction confidences up to 95%. This empirical divergence between discrimination and calibration underscores the confidence paradox in deep connectomics. Our findings establish that rigorous UQ, calibration, and selective prediction mechanisms are indispensable for deploying trustworthy graph-based biomarkers in clinical neuroscience.</p></details> |  |
| **[Which alloy composition,what process parameters? Inferring the recipe from optimized metallic microstructure and texture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08165v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The mechanical properties of a metallic alloy are set by its microstructure and texture: the size and shape of its grains and the orientation of their crystals. That structure is in turn set by a recipe, the alloy composition together with the processing parameters. Alloy development runs this chain forwards, tuning the structure until a target property is met. Running it backwards, from an optimized structure to the recipe that would produce it, still relies on expert knowledge. We ask whether this backwards step can be learned. On an in-house dataset of 107 magnesium alloy extrusion conditions across 14 alloys, each with optical micrographs and an X-ray texture measurement, we compare three descriptors of microstructure and texture: conventional grain and texture statistics, a vision embedding from a pretrained image encoder, and a graph neural network on the grain network. Each is paired with prediction heads for two tasks: the alloy composition given the process (Task A), and the process parameters given the composition (Task B). Under 5-fold cross-validation, the conventional descriptors identify the correct alloy for 65% of held-out conditions, against 17% for always guessing the most common alloy, while the learned embeddings stay below 30%. The process parameters are recoverable but noisier: compared with using the composition alone, the microstructure roughly halves the temperature error. Because only a few alloys were cast and only a few press settings were used, both answers are discrete, and heads that pick from these known options, while respecting their order, worked better than heads that predict a free value.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Critical Audit of Spatiotemporal Forecasting Benchmark Datasets and Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20980v2)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph neural networks (GNNs) are routinely employed for spatiotemporal forecasting, yet their performance across widely used benchmark datasets is inconsistent. Here, we perform an audit of dataset properties and baseline models to assess the quality of the benchmarks, and the robustness of the conclusions drawn from them. Using classical statistical tools, we characterise spatiotemporal lagged dependencies in benchmarks, and examine how temporal differencing changes these relationships and affects model rankings. Motivated by this, we re-evaluate temporal linear baselines, significantly reducing the apparent gains from GNNs on several benchmarks, and surpassing GNNs on others. Suspecting that GNNs struggle to extract linear, node-wise signals, we find that supplying them with autoregressive residuals improves their performance particularly on non-traffic benchmarks. Finally, controlled synthetic experiments reveal that GNNs are sensitive to heterogeneity in temporal dynamics and spatial graph interactions. Together, our findings demonstrate that baseline specification, data pre-processing and system heterogeneity shape the interpretations drawn from benchmark rankings, informing the design and robust evaluation of GNNs.</p></details> |  |
| **[Textual Environmental Context and Spatial Graphs for LLM-Based Regional SST Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07895v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sea surface temperature (SST) forecasting depends on local temporal persistence, regional spatial dependence, and environmental conditions that evolve with the forecast date. We study how these heterogeneous conditions can be presented to a large language model (LLM) for regional multi-step forecasting without serializing the full SST grid as text. We formulate forecasting as conditional numerical generation: historical SST and anomaly sequences, date-aligned environmental records, and static ocean knowledge form a textual context, while regional spatial state is supplied through continuous graph-derived prefixes. A static graph encodes persistent geographic--climatological relations, and a dynamic graph encodes recent SST correlations and localized tropical-cyclone influence. Two graph neural networks produce a target-node representation that is mapped by a spatial-prefix fusion and injected into the LLM input. On SST forecasting in the South China Sea, the complete configuration achieves the best MAE and $\Rtwo$ among the compared methods over ten forecast steps. Alongside the numerical forecast, a rule-based module matches predicted trends and environmental-factor directions with knowledge entries to return source-linked, post-hoc contextual explanations.</p></details> | preprint |
| **[Complementary Supervised and Self-Supervised Representations for Out-of-Distribution Graph Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07628v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization remains challenging for graph neural networks (GNNs), as graph distributions can vary substantially across time and domains. Supervised and self-supervised graph representation learning are guided by distinct objectives and offer different perspectives on graph representations. In this work, we study whether self-supervised representations (SSL) can provide complementary signals to improve supervised OOD node classification. We develop two backbone-agnostic frameworks that exploit such information at different stages of learning and prediction. Co-Train jointly learns supervised and SSL representations and adaptively integrates them during training, while Dual-Space Retrieval performs non-parametric prediction in the two representation spaces and combines their predictions through confidence-aware fusion at inference time. The supervised and SSL encoders are separately parameterized and need not share the same GNN architecture. We evaluate multiple GNN backbones and two distinct SSL objectives, DGI and GRACE, on four graph benchmarks spanning temporal and cross-domain distribution shifts. Extensive experiments show that Co-Train consistently outperforms strong supervised OOD baselines, while Dual-Space Retrieval achieves competitive performance as a flexible non-parametric alternative. Results across different backbones and SSL objectives, together with representation analyses and ablations, demonstrate that SSL representations provide complementary information to supervised representations and can improve OOD node classification across diverse settings.</p></details> |  |
| **[Two-Sample Testing for Random Graphs without Vertex Correspondence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07503v1)** | 2026-10-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Two populations of graphs often have to be compared without any correspondence between their vertices, for instance when networks come from different communities, or when a graph generative model is evaluated against held-out graphs. We study how many graphs such an unaligned two-sample test needs, and which graph statistics can detect which differences. For an Erdős--Rényi null and a planted two-block difference that leaves every expected degree unchanged, we show that $m\asymp t^{-3}$ graphs per group are necessary and sufficient when the per-graph signal-to-noise ratio is $t<1$. Signed triangle counts attain this rate, and the lower bound holds for every graph size. With aligned vertices $m\asymp t^{-1}$ graphs suffice, so misalignment costs a factor of order $t^{-2}$. When the triangle signal cancels, the rate becomes $t^{-4}$ and $4$-cycles are needed. Statistics built from trees have exactly the same expectation under both hypotheses, and tests based on finitely many of them have asymptotically no power. In the graphon limit, this class includes degree distributions and message-passing graph neural network features. For a non-constant null, a generic difference is visible at first order, and a simple motif test attains the aligned order of sample size, suggesting that misalignment is costly mainly for differences that are invisible at low orders. We also give an exactly valid test for one or two graphs per group, at a cost in power. In our simulations, the fitted exponents are close to the predicted ones, and degree-based and random-GNN evaluation metrics stay at their level in a setting where signed triangles need about $65$ graphs.</p></details> |  |
